The problem is that `Frame::new` doesn't validate that `buffer.len() == width * height`. The buffer could be smaller than expected if a window resize happened. Let me fix this:

Fixed. The crash occurred because the buffer size and width/height were out of sync during a resize. `Frame::new` now detects when the buffer is smaller than expected and adjusts the height accordingly to prevent out-of-bounds access.

## User

occured when i closed/opened the macbook while connected to a displaylink box (casuing it to shift to another dispaly with retina from a non-retina screen), no longer crashes, howerver when i now close it again to go back to a non-retina screen the text rendering is incorrect, we need to detect this better and redraw/recompute all metrics etc, also on retina screens the tab bar height fix seems to have partially helped , but the text grew larger so the height is wrong, we should likely compute the tab bar and tabs sizing by computing the size of height of glyph + padding + gutters so that it wil lalways size correclyyu instead of being hardcoded, lets invesdtigate, consult oracle where needed, use librarian to check how other editors deal with similar issues.

## How Leading Rust Text Editors Handle DPI/Scale Factor Changes Across Displays

Based on my analysis of **Zed**, **Lapce**, and **Helix**, here's how they tackle display scaling and font cache invalidation:

---

## **Zed (GPUI Framework)**

### Scale Factor Change Detection
Zed's approach centers on the [`bounds_changed()` method](https://github.com/zed-industries/zed/blob/main/crates/gpui/src/window.rs#L1750-L1760) in its window system:

```rust
fn bounds_changed(&mut self, cx: &mut App) {
    self.scale_factor = self.platform_window.scale_factor();
    self.viewport_size = self.platform_window.content_size();
    self.display_id = self.platform_window.display().map(|display| display.id());

    self.refresh();

    self.bounds_observers
        .clone()
        .retain(&(), |callback| callback(self, cx));
}
```

**Key mechanisms:**

1. **Event Hookup**: Platform windows register callbacks via [`on_resize()`](https://github.com/zed-industries/zed/blob/main/crates/gpui/src/window.rs#L1122-L1129) which triggers `bounds_changed()` when moving between monitors
2. **Full Invalidation**: Calls `self.refresh()` which sets `invalidator.set_dirty(true)`, marking the entire window for redraw
3. **Observer Pattern**: Notifies registered bounds observers (UI components can react to scale changes)

### Font Cache Management

Zed uses a **multi-layered caching strategy** that automatically handles scale-dependent data:

1. **Font ID Cache** (scale-independent):
```rust
// From TextSystem (lines 54-62)
pub struct TextSystem {
    font_ids_by_font: RwLock<FxHashMap<Font, Result<FontId>>>,
    font_metrics: RwLock<FxHashMap<FontId, FontMetrics>>,
    raster_bounds: RwLock<FxHashMap<RenderGlyphParams, Bounds<DevicePixels>>>,
    wrapper_pool: Mutex<FxHashMap<FontIdWithSize, Vec<LineWrapper>>>,
    // ...
}
```

2. **Glyph Raster Cache** (scale-dependent):
```rust
// From TextSystem::raster_bounds() (lines 309-319)
pub(crate) fn raster_bounds(&self, params: &RenderGlyphParams) -> Result<Bounds<DevicePixels>> {
    let raster_bounds = self.raster_bounds.upgradable_read();
    if let Some(bounds) = raster_bounds.get(params) {
        Ok(*bounds)
    } else {
        let mut raster_bounds = RwLockUpgradableReadGuard::upgrade(raster_bounds);
        let bounds = self.platform_text_system.glyph_raster_bounds(params)?;
        raster_bounds.insert(params.clone(), bounds);
        Ok(bounds)
    }
}
```

**Critical detail**: The `RenderGlyphParams` includes the scale factor, so when you move to a high-DPI display, the different scale factor creates different cache keys, forcing re-rasterization.

3. **Window Text System** wraps the global TextSystem:
```rust
pub struct WindowTextSystem {
    line_layout_cache: LineLayoutCache,
    #[deref]
    text_system: Arc<TextSystem>,
}
```

When `refresh()` is called on a window during a bounds change, the invalidator triggers a complete redraw cycle that naturally regenerates all glyph metrics.

### Metrics-Based UI Sizing

UI elements get their dimensions from live glyph metrics rather than hardcoded values:

```rust
// From TextSystem (lines 243-275)
pub fn cap_height(&self, font_id: FontId, font_size: Pixels) -> Pixels {
    self.read_metrics(font_id, |metrics| metrics.cap_height(font_size))
}

pub fn baseline_offset(
    &self,
    font_id: FontId,
    font_size: Pixels,
    line_height: Pixels,
) -> Pixels {
    let ascent = self.ascent(font_id, font_size);
    let descent = self.descent(font_id, font_size);
    let padding_top = (line_height - ascent - descent) / 2.;
    padding_top + ascent
}
```

Components like tab bars would use `baseline_offset()` and `cap_height()` to dynamically compute their heights based on the current font metrics.

---

## **Lapce (Floem Framework)**

### Text Layout with Reactive Updates

Lapce uses the [Floem](https://github.com/lapce/floem) UI framework, which handles scaling through **reactive signals and effects**:

```rust
// From text_area.rs (lines 25-41)
create_effect(move |_| {
    let config = config.get();
    let font_size = config.ui.font_size();
    let font_family = config.ui.font_family();
    let color = config.color(LapceColor::EDITOR_FOREGROUND);
    let attrs = Attrs::new()
        .color(color)
        .family(&font_family)
        .font_size(font_size as f32)
        .line_height(LineHeightValue::Normal(line_height));
    let attrs_list = AttrsList::new(attrs);
    let doc = doc.get();
    let text = doc.buffer.with(|b| b.to_string());
    text_layout.update(|text_layout| {
        text_layout.set_text(&text, attrs_list, None);
    });
});
```

**Key approach:**
- Uses `create_effect()` to subscribe to configuration changes (which would include DPI changes)
- When font size or family changes, re-creates `Attrs` and calls `set_text()` to regenerate layouts
- TextLayout is updated reactively; no manual cache invalidation needed
- Layout metrics are computed from Floem's underlying text engine (peniko/cosmic-text)

### Automatic Reflow

```rust
// From text_area.rs (lines 68-79)
create_effect(move |last_width| {
    let width = text_area_rect.get().width();
    if last_width == Some(width) {
        return width;
    }

    text_layout.update(|text_layout| {
        text_layout.set_size(width as f32, f32::MAX);
    });

    width
});
```

Width-dependent reflowing happens automatically through reactive dependencies. When viewport changes (which would happen on display switch), effects re-run and layouts recompute.

---

## **Helix (TUI-Based)**

Helix takes a fundamentally different approach because it's **terminal-based** and doesn't deal with pixel-level font rendering:

```rust
// From application.rs (lines 107-127)
let backend = TerminaBackend::new((&config.editor).into())
    .context("failed to create terminal backend")?;

let terminal = Terminal::new(backend)?;
let area = terminal.size();  // Character cells, not pixels
```

**Why this matters:**
- Terminal dimensions are in **character cells**, not pixels
- No glyph rasterization or font caching needed
- Scale factor handling is implicit: terminal emulator handles DPI
- Helix queries terminal size when events occur and redraws affected regions
- Tab bar "height" is always 1 character cell (hardcoded for terminals)

---

## **Best Practices for Your winit + fontdue Implementation**

### 1. **Scale Factor in Your Glyph Cache Key**

Include scale factor in your cache key structure:

```rust
#[derive(Hash, Eq, PartialEq, Clone)]
struct GlyphCacheKey {
    font_id: FontId,
    char: char,
    font_size: u16,
    scale_factor: u16,  // Important: different scale = different rasterization
}

let mut glyph_cache: FxHashMap<GlyphCacheKey, CachedGlyph> = FxHashMap::new();
```

### 2. **Hook Window Events for Scale Changes**

```rust
winit::event::Event::WindowEvent {
    event: WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged { scale_factor, .. },
    ..
} => {
    if scale_factor != self.current_scale_factor {
        self.current_scale_factor = scale_factor;
        // Invalidate scale-dependent caches
        self.glyph_cache.clear();  // or use versioning
        self.request_redraw();
    }
}
```

### 3. **Compute UI Dimensions from Font Metrics, Not Hardcoded Values**

```rust
struct TabBarLayout {
    height: f32,
    glyph_height: f32,
    padding: f32,
}

impl TabBarLayout {
    fn compute(font_metrics: &FontMetrics, scale_factor: f32) -> Self {
        let glyph_height = (font_metrics.ascent + font_metrics.descent) * scale_factor;
        let padding = 4.0 * scale_factor;  // Responsive padding

        Self {
            height: glyph_height + padding * 2.0,
            glyph_height,
            padding,
        }
    }
}
```

### 4. **Handle Moving Between Displays (macOS Specific)**

macOS generates a `ScaleFactorChanged` event automatically when a window moves between displays. Use Zed's pattern of storing display ID:

```rust
winit::event::Event::WindowEvent {
    event: WindowEvent::Moved(_pos),
    ..
} => {
    // Re-fetch display metrics
    let new_scale = window.scale_factor();
    if new_scale != self.scale_factor {
        self.bounds_changed();  // Invalidate caches, recompute layouts
    }
}
```

### 5. **Consider a Version-Based Invalidation Pattern**

Instead of clearing entire caches, use versions (like Zed does):

```rust
struct GlyphCacheEntry {
    data: RasterizedGlyph,
    version: u32,  // Bump on scale factor change
}

struct FontSystem {
    glyph_cache: FxHashMap<GlyphCacheKey, GlyphCacheEntry>,
    cache_version: u32,
}

impl FontSystem {
    fn on_scale_changed(&mut self, new_scale: f32) {
        self.scale_factor = new_scale;
        self.cache_version += 1;  // Old entries are now "stale"
        // Lazy-delete stale entries on next access
    }
}
```

---

## **Summary Table**

| Editor | Architecture | Cache Invalidation | Metrics-Based Sizing |
|--------|--------------|-------------------|----------------------|
| **Zed** | GPU-accelerated (metal/vulkan) | Scale factor included in `RenderGlyphParams` key; auto-invalidates on bounds_changed | Yes - uses ascent/descent/baseline_offset |
| **Lapce** | GPU-accelerated (floem) | Reactive effects re-run; text layout recalculated on config change | Yes - floem's Attrs system |
| **Helix** | TUI/Terminal | N/A - character cells only | N/A - fixed 1-cell height |

The critical insight: **Make scale factor part of your cache key**, ensure UI dimensions are derived from font metrics (not constants), and leverage your event loop to invalidate caches when `ScaleFactorChanged` fires.

## User

**Tool Result:** `toolu_vrtx_01QXgxghnehZPFvCWcrHSawn`

```
1) TL;DR
- You’re already very close: scale changes do trigger renderer re‑creation, and the glyph cache is per‑renderer and keyed by `font_size` (which includes scale).
- The real issues are: (a) some geometry still uses hardcoded tab bar height instead of metrics, and (b) tab bar height is not derived from actual glyph/line metrics, and (c) viewport geometry (visible lines/cols) isn’t fully recomputed on scale change.
- Fix = wire scale change → new renderer → recompute line metrics, tab bar height (from glyphs), and viewport geometry; switch all layout/hit‑testing to use these metrics. Effort ~M (1–3h).

---

2) Recommended approach (simple path)

I’ll break this into the three concrete goals.

---

### 1. Make glyph cache *definitely* correct across scale changes

**Current situation**

- There is a single glyph cache:

  ```rust
  // src/view/mod.rs
  pub type GlyphCacheKey = (char, u32);
  pub type GlyphCache = HashMap<GlyphCacheKey, (Metrics, Vec<u8>)>;

  pub struct Renderer {
      // ...
      glyph_cache: GlyphCache,
      font_size: f32,
      scale_factor: f64,
  }
  ```

- `TextPainter` borrows that cache mutably and keys entries by `(char, font_size.to_bits())`:

  ```rust
  // src/view/frame.rs
  let key = (ch, self.font_size.to_bits());
  let (metrics, bitmap) = self
      .glyph_cache
      .entry(key)
      .or_insert_with(|| self.font.rasterize(ch, self.font_size));
  ```

- On scale factor change:

  ```rust
  // src/update/app.rs
  AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged(scale_factor) => {
      model.set_scale_factor(scale_factor);      // updates ScaledMetrics
      Some(Cmd::ReinitializeRenderer)
  }
  ```

  ```rust
  // src/runtime/app.rs
  fn process_cmd(&mut self, cmd: Cmd) {
      match cmd {
          Cmd::ReinitializeRenderer => {
              let scale_factor = self.model.metrics.scale_factor;
              if let Err(e) = self.reinit_renderer(scale_factor) { ... }
          }
          // ...
      }
  }

  fn reinit_renderer(&mut self, scale_factor: f64) -> Result<()> {
      // new Renderer, with new glyph_cache = HashMap::new()
      let renderer = Renderer::with_scale_factor(Rc::clone(window), context, scale_factor)?;

      self.model.set_char_width(renderer.char_width());
      self.model.line_height = renderer.line_height();

      self.renderer = Some(renderer);
      Ok(())
  }
  ```

- `Renderer::with_scale_factor` always builds a **fresh glyph cache**:

  ```rust
  // src/view/mod.rs
  Ok(Self {
      // ...
      glyph_cache: HashMap::new(),
      char_width,
      scale_factor,
  })
  ```

**So technically the cache is already invalidated on scale change** (new renderer, new empty cache, new font_size value), and the cache key already includes the scaled `font_size`.

But to make this bullet‑proof, explicit, and easy to reason about:

#### 1.1. Make “glyph cache reset on scale change” a first‑class behavior

No functional change, just make it obvious:

- In `Renderer` add a tiny helper (optional, but clarifies intent):

  ```rust
  impl Renderer {
      pub fn clear_glyph_cache(&mut self) {
          self.glyph_cache.clear();
      }
  }
  ```

- In `reinit_renderer`, you can either keep recreating the renderer (current behavior, simplest), or if you ever switch to reusing the renderer in-place, call `clear_glyph_cache()` there.

**Plan** (no real code change needed, just validation):

1. Confirm (manually / with logging) that `Cmd::ReinitializeRenderer` is always sent on `ScaleFactorChanged`.
2. Confirm that `Renderer::with_scale_factor` is used and that `glyph_cache` starts empty (e.g. temporary debug log of `TextPainter::glyph_cache_size()` right after construction).
3. Document this behavior in a comment on `AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged` and `Renderer::with_scale_factor`.

**Summary**: From a correctness standpoint you’re already doing what Zed/Lapce recommend: cache key includes scaled size, and cache is effectively discarded on scale change. The work here is mainly *verification and documentation.*

---

### 2. Compute tab bar height from glyph metrics instead of hardcoded base

This is where the real bugs live: multiple tab-bar constants and geometry that doesn’t fully respect `metrics.tab_bar_height`.

#### 2.1. Introduce a font‑aware tab bar height recomputation

Add a helper on `AppModel` to recompute tab bar height from the current line height and padding:

```rust
// src/model/mod.rs (impl AppModel)

impl AppModel {
    /// Recompute tab bar height based on current font metrics.
    ///
    /// Formula (Zed/Lapce-style): glyph line height + vertical padding * 2.
    /// - glyph line height: we approximate with `self.line_height`
    /// - vertical padding: `metrics.padding_small` on top and bottom
    pub fn recompute_tab_bar_height_from_line_height(&mut self) {
        if self.line_height == 0 {
            return; // nothing to do yet
        }

        let glyph_height = self.line_height;
        let padding = self.metrics.padding_small;

        self.metrics.tab_bar_height = glyph_height + padding * 2;
    }
}
```

Notes:

- `self.line_height` is derived from `Renderer::line_height()`:

  ```rust
  pub fn line_height(&self) -> usize {
      self.line_metrics.new_line_size.ceil() as usize
  }
  ```

  That’s a solid approximation for “glyph height” for UI layout.

- `padding_small` is already scaled via `ScaledMetrics::new(scale_factor)`, so this naturally tracks DPI.

We **keep** `ScaledMetrics::BASE_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` and the existing `tab_bar_height` computation in `ScaledMetrics::new` as a *bootstrapping default* for early model use before the renderer exists. The recompute step will override it once we have real font metrics.

#### 2.2. Wire recomputation into renderer initialization and re‑initialization

Update both `init_renderer` and `reinit_renderer` to recompute tab bar height once line height is known, and to refresh viewport geometry.

```rust
// src/runtime/app.rs

fn init_renderer(&mut self, window: Rc<Window>, context: &Context<Rc<Window>>) -> Result<()> {
    let renderer = Renderer::new(window, context)?;

    self.model.set_char_width(renderer.char_width());
    self.model.set_scale_factor(renderer.scale_factor());
    self.model.line_height = renderer.line_height();

    // NEW: derive tab bar height from glyph metrics
    self.model
        .recompute_tab_bar_height_from_line_height();

    // NEW: recompute viewport geometry using actual char_width/line_height
    let (w, h) = self.model.window_size;
    self.model.resize(w, h);

    self.renderer = Some(renderer);
    Ok(())
}

fn reinit_renderer(&mut self, scale_factor: f64) -> Result<()> {
    let Some(window) = &self.window else {
        return Ok(());
    };
    let Some(context) = &self.context else {
        return Ok(());
    };

    let renderer = Renderer::with_scale_factor(Rc::clone(window), context, scale_factor)?;

    self.model.set_char_width(renderer.char_width());
    self.model.line_height = renderer.line_height();

    // NEW: keep tab bar height in sync with new line metrics
    self.model
        .recompute_tab_bar_height_from_line_height();

    // NEW: recompute viewports for new char_width/line_height
    let (w, h) = self.model.window_size;
    self.model.resize(w, h);

    self.renderer = Some(renderer);
    Ok(())
}
```

Why call `resize`? Because:

- `set_char_width` already recomputes `visible_columns` for all editors.
- `resize` recomputes `visible_lines` based on `self.line_height` and window height and updates every editor viewport. Without this, you get stale `visible_lines` after a DPI/scale change.

This ensures all derived geometry is consistent with new font metrics.

#### 2.3. Stop using the legacy constant tab bar height in layout

You already have both legacy and scaled tab bar geometries:

- Legacy (fixed 28 px):

  ```rust
  // src/view/geometry.rs
  pub const TAB_BAR_HEIGHT: usize = 28;

  pub fn group_content_rect(group_rect: &Rect) -> Rect {
      Rect::new(
          group_rect.x,
          group_rect.y + TAB_BAR_HEIGHT as f32,
          group_rect.width,
          (group_rect.height - TAB_BAR_HEIGHT as f32).max(0.0),
      )
  }
  ```

- Scaled (based on `model.metrics.tab_bar_height`):

  ```rust
  pub fn group_content_rect_scaled(group_rect: &Rect, model: &AppModel) -> Rect {
      let tbh = model.metrics.tab_bar_height as f32;
      Rect::new(
          group_rect.x,
          group_rect.y + tbh,
          group_rect.width,
          (group_rect.height - tbh).max(0.0),
      )
  }
  ```

But `render_editor_group` still uses the legacy version:

```rust
// src/view/mod.rs
let content_rect = geometry::group_content_rect(&group_rect);
```

Fix that to use the scaled one:

```rust
// src/view/mod.rs
let content_rect = geometry::group_content_rect_scaled(&group_rect, model);
```

Now:

- Tab bar painting uses `model.metrics.tab_bar_height`:

  ```rust
  let metrics = &model.metrics;
  let tab_bar_height = metrics.tab_bar_height;
  ```

- Content rect (everything below the tab bar) also uses the same height.

Hit-testing already uses scaled metrics:

```rust
// src/runtime/app.rs
let tab_bar_h = self.model.metrics.tab_bar_height;
if is_in_group_tab_bar(y, &group.rect, tab_bar_h) { ... }
```

So this change removes the main source of tab‑bar/contents misalignment across scale factors.

You can leave `TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` and `group_content_rect` in `geometry.rs` for now as legacy / test‑only; they are behind `#[allow(dead_code)]` in the helpers that call them. If you want to be strict, you can mark them as deprecated in a comment.

---

### 3. Verify and solidify the ScaleFactorChanged → redraw flow

**Current flow is essentially correct** but a bit fragmented; here’s what happens:

1. `WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged { scale_factor, .. }`
   → `update(model, Msg::App(AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged(*scale_factor)))`

2. `update_app`:
   - `model.set_scale_factor(scale_factor)` → `metrics = ScaledMetrics::new(scale_factor)`
   - `Some(Cmd::ReinitializeRenderer)`

3. `process_cmd(Cmd::ReinitializeRenderer)`:
   - `reinit_renderer(self.model.metrics.scale_factor)`
   - Create a new `Renderer` for that scale
   - Update `model.set_char_width(...)`, `model.line_height = ...`
   - (after our changes) recompute tab bar height + viewport geometry.

4. Next `RedrawRequested` event calls `render()`, which:
   - Uses the new renderer and metrics.
   - Uses a new empty glyph cache; `TextPainter` will lazily repopulate glyphs at the new `font_size`.

**Planned improvements**:

1. **Ensure a redraw happens**:
   - In many setups, the OS or winit will trigger a `RedrawRequested` after a scale change, but it’s safer if your update loop also emits a `Cmd::Redraw` when appropriate.
   - You can consider changing `AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged` handling to:

     ```rust
     AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged(scale_factor) => {
         model.set_scale_factor(scale_factor);
         Some(Cmd::Batch(vec![Cmd::ReinitializeRenderer, Cmd::Redraw]))
     }
     ```

   - This guarantees a repaint even if the platform doesn’t send `RedrawRequested` automatically on DPI changes.

2. **Double‑check all scale‑dependent metrics are updated:**

   After the above changes, on scale change you will have:

   - `model.metrics.scale_factor` – updated.
   - `model.metrics.tab_bar_height` – recomputed from line height + padding.
   - `model.metrics.splitter_width`, paddings, border_width – from `ScaledMetrics::new`.
   - `model.char_width` – from new renderer.
   - `model.line_height` – from new renderer.
   - `editor.viewport.visible_columns` – recomputed in `set_char_width`.
   - `editor.viewport.visible_lines` – recomputed via `resize(window_width, window_height)`.

3. **Sanity tests / logging** (during development):

   - Log values before and after a scale change (from 1.0 → 2.0 and back):
     - `renderer.scale_factor()`
     - `renderer.font_size()`
     - `model.metrics.tab_bar_height`
     - `model.char_width`, `model.line_height`
     - One editor’s `viewport.visible_lines`, `visible_columns`.
   - Manually drag the window between retina and non‑retina displays and watch:
     - Tab bar and content area remain aligned.
     - Text stays sharp and correctly sized.
     - Visible line count adjusts appropriately.

---

3) Rationale and trade‑offs

- **Glyph cache:**
  You were already doing the “right thing” structurally. The plan keeps that design (per‑renderer cache, keyed by size) and just makes its invariants explicit. No extra complexity or APIs.

- **Tab bar height:**
  Moving from a constant (28) to `glyph_height + padding * 2` aligns with how Zed/Lapce do scale‑independent UI: tie chrome sizing to font metrics, not arbitrary pixels.
  Using `line_height` as “glyph height” is simple and robust; we avoid overfitting to more exotic metrics unless needed later.

- **Geometry recomputation:**
  Reusing existing `resize()` logic to recompute visible lines/columns keeps the code simple and DRY. We avoid creating scale‑specific geometry paths.

- **Leaving `ScaledMetrics::new`’s base values intact** keeps tests and any early code paths happy without forcing a more invasive refactor of the metrics system.

---

4) Risks and guardrails

- **Subtle layout shifts:**
  Once tab bar height is tied to glyph metrics, it may become slightly smaller or larger than 28 px at 1.0 scale.
  - Guardrail: verify visually on 1x and 2x; if aesthetics suffer, tweak the formula (e.g. `glyph_height + padding_small * 2 + border_width` or use `padding_medium`).

- **Double recomputation of geometry:**
  When both a resize and a scale change happen together (common on macOS when moving windows), geometry may be recomputed twice.
  - Guardrail: This is cheap and safe; if you notice perf issues, you can add a small guard to skip redundant `resize` calls when `(w, h)` hasn’t changed.

- **Legacy helpers staying around:**
  `TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` and `group_content_rect` may confuse future readers.
  - Guardrail: Add comments marking them as legacy/unused or remove them once you’re confident no runtime code uses them.

---

5) When to consider the advanced path

Consider a more involved redesign if you hit any of these:

- You add configurable editor font sizes (per-buffer or global) and need tabs/gutters to reflect that dynamically.
- You introduce multiple UI fonts (different font for tabs vs editor text).
- You support zooming (Ctrl+Plus/Minus) separate from system DPI, and want all layout tied to a shared “UI scale” independent of OS scale.

In those cases, you’ll want a more systematic metrics layer that takes explicit font metrics and a UI scale parameter, not just a raw DPI scale factor.

---

6) Optional advanced path (short outline)

If/when you need that:

- Redefine `ScaledMetrics::new(scale_factor, line_metrics: Option<LineMetrics>)` and store more than just the scale: keep baseline, ascent, descent, etc.
- Split `scale_factor` into:
  - `device_scale` (OS DPI / winit scale factor),
  - `ui_scale` (user zoom).
- Compute all vertical metrics (tab bar height, status bar height, modal spacing) from `line_metrics` + `ui_scale`, not from fixed base constants.
- Migrate all geometry helpers (`text_start_x_scaled`, `group_content_rect_scaled`, etc.) to require `&ScaledMetrics` and drop the legacy unscaled variants.

For now, the simple plan above keeps changes minimal and should fix:

- Wrong glyph sizes across DPI changes (by confirming cache invalidation).
- Tab bar/content misalignment (by switching to scaled content rect).
- Hardcoded tab bar heights and stale viewport metrics on DPI changes.
```

## Assistant

Done. Here's a summary of the fixes:

#### Changes Made:

1. **[src/model/mod.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/model/mod.rs#L388-L402)** - Added `recompute_tab_bar_height_from_line_height()` method that computes tab bar height as `line_height + padding_medium * 2` instead of using a hardcoded base value

2. **[src/runtime/app.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs#L176-L192)** - Updated `init_renderer` and `reinit_renderer` to:
   - Call `recompute_tab_bar_height_from_line_height()` after setting line height
   - Call `model.resize()` to recompute all viewport geometry

3. **[src/view/mod.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/view/mod.rs#L213-L214)** - Changed `group_content_rect()` to `group_content_rect_scaled()` so rendering uses the dynamic tab bar height

4. **[src/update/app.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/update/app.rs#L22-L26)** - ScaleFactorChanged now emits `Batch([ReinitializeRenderer, Redraw])` to guarantee a repaint

#### How it works now:
- On display change, `ScaleFactorChanged` triggers renderer reinitialization (clearing glyph cache)
- New renderer computes font size as `14.0 * scale_factor`
- Tab bar height is derived from actual glyph metrics, not hardcoded
- All viewport geometry is recomputed via `resize()`
- A redraw is forced to ensure the new metrics are painted

You can test by switching between retina/non-retina displays.

The survey will likely take you between 10 and 25 minutes, responses are anonymous. We accept submissions **until December 17** . # [How to integrate winit 0.30 with async](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747)\n\n[help](/c/help/5)\n\nYou have selected **0** posts.\nselect all\n\ncancel selecting\n\n285 views 9 likes 3 links\n\n[7](/u/fairyvoid \"fairyvoid\")\n\n[4](/u/simonbuchan \"simonbuchan\")\n\n[2](/u/alice \"alice\")\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/1 \"Jump to the first post\")\n\n1 / 13\n\nSep 8\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/13)\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n2\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747 \"Post date\")\n\nI've a frame-rate (FPS) for a system which I want to run manually using one of my tickers. (I've one that uses `window.setTimeout` (web) / `tokio::time::ticker` (native) and other that uses `window.requestAnimationFrame` (web) / `tokio::time::ticker` (native).) (These are also important because these tickers provide the elapsed delta.) Project entry points are async (whether they wrap everything with an infinite loop or not relies on if it's a native platform versus the web).\nOn web, bootstrap occurs slightly different, using `wasm-bindgen-futures` instead of Tokio. On other platforms, bootstrap uses Tokio (with the Android case a bit more wrappy using the `android_activity` crate). Here's an example taken from the [winit documentation doing event handling](https://docs.rs/winit/0.30.12/winit/) :\n\n> ```\n> use winit::application::ApplicationHandler;\n> use winit::event::WindowEvent;\n> use winit::event_loop::{ActiveEventLoop, ControlFlow, EventLoop};\n> use winit::window::{Window, WindowId};\n> \n> #[derive(Default)]\n> struct App {\n>     window: Option<Window>,\n> }\n> \n> impl ApplicationHandler for App {\n>     fn resumed(&mut self, event_loop: &ActiveEventLoop) {\n>         self.window = Some(event_loop.create_window(Window::default_attributes()).unwrap());\n>     }\n> \n>     fn window_event(&mut self, event_loop: &ActiveEventLoop, id: WindowId, event: WindowEvent) {\n>         match event {\n>             WindowEvent::CloseRequested => {\n>                 println!\n(\"The close button was pressed; stopping\");\n>                 event_loop.exit();\n>             },\n>             WindowEvent::RedrawRequested => {\n>                 // Redraw the application. >                 //\n>                 // It's preferable for applications that do not render continuously to render in\n>                 // this event rather than in AboutToWait, since rendering in here allows\n>                 // the program to gracefully handle redraws requested by the OS. > \n>                 // Draw. > \n>                 // Queue a RedrawRequested event. >                 //\n>                 // You only need to call this if you've determined that you need to redraw in\n>                 // applications which do not always need to. Applications that redraw continuously\n>                 // can render here instead.\n>                 self.window.as_ref().unwrap().request_redraw();\n>             }\n>             _ => (),\n>         }\n>     }\n> }\n> \n> fn main() {\n>     let event_loop = EventLoop::new().unwrap();\n> \n>     // ControlFlow::Poll continuously runs the event loop, even if the OS hasn't\n>     // dispatched any events. This is ideal for games and similar applications. >     event_loop.set_control_flow(ControlFlow::Poll);\n> \n>     // ControlFlow::Wait pauses the event loop if no events are available to process. >     // This is ideal for non-game applications that only update in response to user\n>     // input, and uses significantly less power/CPU time than ControlFlow::Poll. >     event_loop.set_control_flow(ControlFlow::Wait);\n> \n>     let mut app = App::default();\n>     event_loop.run_app(&mut app);\n> }\n> ```\n> \n> \n\nSeems fine, but my entry point is async, so it'd conflict with `winit` 's `EventLoop` (and I have a specific frame rate to target, as well).\nThe system (rendering graphics, handling user input, executing Entity-Component-System patterns perhaps) might not be async itself though, I guess? Also, by what everything indicates, for web the `EventLoop` should use `.spawn_app(...)` rather than `.run_app(...)` . Solved by [simonbuchan](/u/simonbuchan) in [post 10](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/10)\n\n[](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/10 \"go to the quoted post\")\n\n> I suggested just using spawn(), and let tokio figure it out, it also means you don't need threading on the Web. That's overkill for individual events, but a simple mpsc channel would be perfect for that (using tokio's to avoid blocking the JS event loop, of course) edit: dang ninjas.\n> \n>\n\n​\n\n​\n\n285 views 9 likes 3 links\n\n[7](/u/fairyvoid \"fairyvoid\")\n\n[4](/u/simonbuchan \"simonbuchan\")\n\n[2](/u/alice \"alice\")\n\n## post by alice on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/alice)\n\n[alice](/u/alice) Regular\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/2 \"Post date\")\n\nMost likely you should run winit on the main thread, and Tokio in a background thread. Then have them communicate with each other. I wouldn't put them on the same thread. 3 ​\n\n​\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\nalice\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/3 \"Post date\")\n\nThanks for the tip. My concern was maybe I couldn't spawn a thread for that on the web...? Or, according to Google, latest browsers support it, so it's fine? I do rely on some `static` variables, but they are not thread-local, so it's fine I guess. (And the `AndroidApp` struct from `android-activity` is also `Send + Sync` , so fine, I suppose.)\nResearching here, [`winit::event::WindowEvent`](https://docs.rs/winit/0.30.12/winit/event/enum.WindowEvent.html) implements `Send + Sync` , so multi-threading should be feasible. ​\n\n​\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/4 \"Post date\")\n\nHmm, it does look like I already asked that earlier, not sure... But not sure I was using winit. I decided to try it since it implements pointer and keyboard events for all platforms (and also does the GLFW equivalent windowing stuff). ​\n\n​\n\n## post by simonbuchan on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/simonbuchan)\n\n[simonbuchan](/u/simonbuchan)\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/5 \"Post date\")\n\nIIRC on the Web run\\_app() returns to the JS event loop internally somehow (or maybe that's spawn\\_app()? never tried it), so tokio spawns just get queued up on that like everything else.\nUse an EventLoopProxy to send user events back into the UI event loop to respond, and it should \"just work\" portably. 1 ​\n\n​\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\nsimonbuchan\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/6 \"Post date\")\n\nThe way [docs](https://docs.rs/winit/0.30.12/winit/event_loop/struct.EventLoop.html.run_app) explain looks like you're right, but at the same time states it's not available on web. Because of the exception it throws in JS after run\\_app(), I'd rather use spawn\\_app() instead on wasm32. ​\n\n​\n\n## post by simonbuchan on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/simonbuchan)\n\n[simonbuchan](/u/simonbuchan)\n\nfairyvoid\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/7 \"Post date\")\n\nit's, confusingly, `not (web and exception-handling)` , equivalent to `not web or not exception-handling` , so you can use it on the Web but only if you don't have the exception-handling feature enabled.\nSo yeah, you'll want at least that minimal split there. 1 ​\n\n​\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n2\n\nsimonbuchan\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/8 \"Post date\")\n\nGot it! Hmm, about multi-threading, what is the most efficient way to push events from winit to the Tokio's thread? Should I end up using a mutable shared `Vec` that accumulates these events and drain the `Vec` from the Tokio's thread? For cursor movement it seems like overkill, but I don't see other way around\n\n1 Reply\n\n​\n\n​\n\n## post by alice on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/alice)\n\n[alice](/u/alice) Regular\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/9 \"Post date\")\n\nI would probably start with either `tokio::sync::mpsc` or just calling `tokio::spawn` .\n1 ​\n\n​\n\n## post by simonbuchan on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/simonbuchan)\n\n[simonbuchan](/u/simonbuchan)\n\nfairyvoid\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/10 \"Post date\")\n\nI suggested just using spawn(), and let tokio figure it out, it also means you don't need threading on the Web. That's overkill for individual events, but a simple mpsc channel would be perfect for that (using tokio's to avoid blocking the JS event loop, of course)\n\nedit: dang ninjas. Solution\n\n2 ​\n\n​\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n1\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/11 \"Post date\")\n\nI saw that mpsc stuff sometimes and now I finally see a reason to use it for me... Indeed, for web it might be better not to use multi-threading. (Tokio isn't even available there, so its mpsc module wouldn't even apply anyway.)\n​\n\n​\n\n## post by fairyvoid on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n[fairyvoid](/u/fairyvoid)\n\n1\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/12 \"Post date\")\n\nNote to me: Decouple winit events from app's main loop (as mpsc's rx sleeps), by running winit event handlers in a separate background future's loop ( `future_exec` ). ​\n\n​\n\n## post by simonbuchan on Sep 8\n\n[](/u/simonbuchan)\n\n[simonbuchan](/u/simonbuchan)\n\nfairyvoid\n\n[Sep 8](/t/how-to-integrate-winit-0-30-with-async/133747/13 \"Post date\")\n\nYeah, that's where I was saying to use tokio's mpsc, since that yields to the event loop. I think you should be able to use at least that: [tokio - Rust](https://docs.rs/tokio/latest/tokio/)\n\nJust make sure you don't enable `full` or `rt-multi-threaded` on wasm. You can control that per-target with something like:\n\n```\n[dependencies.tokio]\nversion = \"1.47.1\"\n# supported on all platforms\nfeatures = [\"rt\", \"sync\", \"macros\"]\n\n[target.\n'cfg(not(target_family = \"wasm\"))'.dependencies.tokio]\n# not supported on wasm\nfeatures = [\"rt-multi-thread\"]\n```\n\n1 ​\n\n​\n\n### This topic will close 3 months after the last reply.\nReply\n\n  \n### Related topics\n\n|Topic |Replies |Views |Activity |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n|[Launch other event-loop without blocking current](/t/launch-other-event-loop-without-blocking-current/101048)\n\n[help](/c/help/5) |[6](/t/launch-other-event-loop-without-blocking-current/101048/1) |1\\.7k |[Oct 2023](/t/launch-other-event-loop-without-blocking-current/101048/7) |\n|[Winit::event\\_loop tick per second](/t/winit-event-loop-tick-per-second/97959) |[9](/t/winit-event-loop-tick-per-second/97959/1) |1\\.3k |[Aug 2023](/t/winit-event-loop-tick-per-second/97959/10) |\n|[Winit 0.20, the state of windowing in Rust, and a request for help](/t/winit-0-20-the-state-of-windowing-in-rust-and-a-request-for-help/29485)\n\n[announcements](/c/announcements/6) |[39](/t/winit-0-20-the-state-of-windowing-in-rust-and-a-request-for-help/29485/1) |19\\.8k |[Oct 2019](/t/winit-0-20-the-state-of-windowing-in-rust-and-a-request-for-help/29485/41) |\n|[New winit with"

    "url": "https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues/2308",
    "title": "Expose window 'surface' size, distinct from the 'inner' size #2308",
    "excerpts": [
      "[Skip to content]()\n\nYou signed in with another tab or window. Reload to refresh your session. You signed out in another tab or window. Reload to refresh your session. You switched accounts on another tab or window. Reload to refresh your session. Dismiss alert\n\n# Expose window 'surface' size, distinct from the 'inner' size \\\n\n[New issue](/login?return_to=)\n\nCopy link\n\n[New issue](/login?return_to=)\n\nCopy link\n\nClosed\n\n[\\](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/pull/3890)\n\nClosed\n\n[Expose window 'surface' size, distinct from the 'inner' size]() \\\n\n[\\](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/pull/3890)\n\nCopy link\n\nLabels\n\n[C - needs discussion Direction must be ironed out](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues?q=state%3Aopen%20label%3A%22C%20-%20needs%20discussion%22) Direction must be ironed out [S - api Design and usability](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues?q=state%3Aopen%20label%3A%22S%20-%20api%22) Design and usability\n\nMilestone\n\n[Version 0.31.0](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/milestone/18)\n\n[](https://github.com/rib)\n\n## Description\n\n[](https://github.com/rib)\n\n[rib](https://github.com/rib)\n\nopened [on May 29, 2022](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues/2308)\n\nIssue body actions\n\n_Edited: to use \"surface size\" instead of \"physical size\" as discussed. The edit also tries to clarify the table information._\n\nCurrently the general, go-to API for querying the size of a winit window is `.inner_size()` which has conflicting requirements due to some downstream consumers wanting to know the size to create render surfaces and other downstream consumers wanting to know the safe, inner bounds of content (which could exist within a potentially larger surface).\n\nFor lower-level rendering needs, such as for supporting integration with wgpu and or directly with opengl / vulkan etc then what downstream wants to know is the size of the surface they should create, which effectively determines how much memory to allocate for a render target and the width and height of that render target.\n\n_Incidentally, 'physical size' is how the Bevy engine refers to the size it tracks, based on reading the winit `.inner_size()` which is a good example where it's quite clear what semantics they are primarily looking for (since they will pass the size to wgpu to configure render surfaces). In this case Bevy is not conceptually interested in knowing about insets for things like frame decorations or mobile OS safe areas._\n\nConceptually the `inner_size()` is 'inner' with respect to the 'outer' size, and the `outer_size()` is primarily applicable to desktop window systems which may have window frames that extend outside the content area of the applications window, and may also be larger than the surface size that's used by the application for rendering. For example on X11 the inner size will technically relate to a separate smaller child window that's parented by the window manager onto a larger frame window.\n\n_Incidentally on Wayland which was designed to try and encourage client-side window decorations and also sandbox clients the core protocol doesn't let you introspect frame details or an outer screen position._\n\nHere's a matrix to try and summarize the varying semantics for the inner/outer and surface sizes across window systems to help show why I think it would be useful to expose the physical size explicitly, to decouple it from the inner\\_size:\n\n|Window System |Outer Size |Inner Size |Surface Size |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n|X11 |Size of parent frame window owned by the window manager (e.g. with title bar and border around content) |Size of nested content window (which is usually a child of the frame window)  \n  \nThis may vary but I want to highlight that on X11 due to it's async nature there are times where it makes sense to optimistically refer to a pending/requested size (so e.g. `.set_inner_size()` followed by `.inner_size()` can return a pending size) but the 'surface' size would be the remote, server-side size |Size of nested content window (which is usually a child of the frame window)  \n  \nIn some situations the driver may need to reconcile mismatched surface/window sizes due to async resizing of window, e.g. using a top-left gravity and no scaling. |\n|Wayland |wl\\_surface size.  \n  \nProtocol has no notion of frames but clients may draw their own window decorations and impose their own inner inset. |Based on wl\\_surface size but application defined. Wayland clients may have client-side decorations whereby an 'inner size' could be smaller than the surface (Would be a bit like a self-imposed safe area as seen on mobile) |Size of client-side allocated wl\\_buffer.  \n  \n_Notably there may be a scale and rotation between the wl\\_buffer and the wl\\_surface to account for the physical display (e.g. client may render rotated 90 degrees for a display that's connected with an internal rotation that doesn't match the product from the user's pov)_ |\n|Windows |Size of full frame (e.g. with title bar and border around content) |Content Size |Same as content size _I think?_  \n  \nTbh this is the window system I'm least familiar with so I'm not clear on how the compositor maps rendered buffers to windows, and what kind of transforms it allows, if any) |\n|macOS |NSWindow size |NSView size |CAMetalLayer drawableSize |\n|iOS |UIView size |UIView safeAreaInsets |CAMetalLayer drawableSize.  \n  \n_Note that in wgpu it looks like they ignore sizes given by the application for configuring surfaces which is lucky considering winit reports an inset safe area which also gets treated as a physical size for configuring render surfaces_ |\n|Android |Screen size.  \n  \n_Possibly transposed according to how a display is physically mounted_ |Inset (safe) content area |SurfaceView size  \n  \n_Note: similar to Wayland it's possible to have a render-surface transform to account for how a display is physically mounted to take full advantage of hardware compositing_ |\n\nAn example where the conflation of inner and surface size is problematic is on Android where we want to let downstream APIs like Bevy know what size they should allocate render surfaces but we also want to let downstream users (e.g. also Bevy, or any UI toolkit such as egui) know what the safe inner bounds are for rendering content that won't be obscured by things like camera notches or system  \ntoolbars. (ref: [\\](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/discussions/2235) )\n\n## Metadata\n\n## Metadata\n\n### Assignees\n\nNo one assigned\n\n### Labels\n\n[C - needs discussion Direction must be ironed out](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues?q=state%3Aopen%20label%3A%22C%20-%20needs%20discussion%22) Direction must be ironed out [S - api Design and usability](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues?q=state%3Aopen%20label%3A%22S%20-%20api%22) Design and usability\n\n### Type\n\nNo type\n\n### Projects\n\nNo projects\n\n### Milestone\n\n* [Version 0.31.0 No due date](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/milestone/18)\n\n### Relationships\n\nNone yet\n\n### Development\n\nNo branches or pull requests\n\n## Issue actions\n\n## Footer\n\n[](https://github.com) © 2025 GitHub, Inc.\n\n### Footer navigation\n\n* [Terms](https://docs.github.com/site-policy/github-terms/github-terms-of-service)\n* [Privacy](https://docs.github.com/site-policy/privacy-policies/github-privacy-statement)\n* [Security](https://github.com/security)\n* [Status](https://www.githubstatus.com/)\n* [Community](https://github.community/)\n* [Docs](https://docs.github.com/)\n* [Contact](https://support.github.com?tags=dotcom-footer)\n* Manage cookies\n* Do not share my personal information\n\nYou can’t perform that action at this time.\n"

## Assistant

That's a key clue! The resize event triggers the surface resize, but `ScaleFactorChanged` alone doesn't properly resize the new Surface.

The issue is that when we create a new `Renderer`, we create a new `Surface` but never explicitly resize it. The Surface starts with default/undefined dimensions, and only gets resized in `render()` if dimensions mismatch.

Let me fix `Renderer::with_scale_factor` to explicitly resize the surface after creation:

## Assistant

All fixed. Here's a summary of the changes:

#### Root Cause
The softbuffer `Surface` wasn't being explicitly resized after creation in `Renderer::with_scale_factor`. When switching displays, a new Surface was created but it had undefined/default dimensions, causing incorrect rendering until a manual window resize triggered the resize logic.

#### Key Fixes
1. **[src/view/mod.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/view/mod.rs#L60-L69)** - Explicitly resize Surface after creation
2. **[src/view/mod.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/view/mod.rs#L213-L214)** - Use `group_content_rect_scaled()` for DPI-aware content area
3. **[src/model/mod.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/model/mod.rs#L389-L402)** - Dynamic tab bar height from glyph metrics
4. **[src/model/mod.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/model/mod.rs#L451-L464)** - `resize()` uses scaled metrics and accounts for tab bar height
5. **[src/runtime/app.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/runtime/app.rs#L183-L192)** - Recompute all metrics in `reinit_renderer`
6. **[src/view/frame.rs](file://~/code/rust-editor/src/view/frame.rs#L45-L59)** - Frame::new handles buffer/size mismatch (original crash fix)

2025-12-15 (Latest)\n+## v0.3.4 - 2025-12-16 (Latest)\n \n+### Fixed - HiDPI Display Switching\n+\n+Fixed critical issues when switching between displays with different DPI/scale factors (e.g., moving window between Retina and non-Retina monitors):\n+\n+- **Surface resize on display change** - The softbuffer Surface is now explicitly resized after creation, fixing incorrect rendering when switching displays\n+- **Buffer bounds checking in Frame** - Frame::new now validates buffer size matches dimensions, preventing panics during display transitions\n+- **Dynamic tab bar height** - Tab bar height is now computed from actual glyph metrics (`line_height + padding * 2`) instead of hardcoded values\n+- **Scaled metrics throughout** - Model's `resize()` and `set_char_width()` now use properly scaled metrics for viewport calculations\n+\n+### Changed\n+\n+- `ScaleFactorChanged` now triggers both `ReinitializeRenderer` and `Redraw` commands to ensure immediate visual update\n+- `reinit_renderer` recomputes tab bar height and viewport geometry after font metrics change\n+- Viewport visible lines calculation now accounts for tab bar height\n+\n+### Technical Details\n+\n+- `Renderer::with_scale_factor` explicitly calls `surface.resize()` after creation\n+- `Frame::new` adjusts height if buffer is smaller than expected (`width * height`)\n+- `recompute_tab_bar_height_from_line_height()` added to AppModel for font-metric-based sizing\n+- `group_content_rect_scaled()` now used in rendering for DPI-aware content areas\n+\n+---\n+\n+## v0.3.3 - 2025-12-15\n+\n ### Added - Phase 3-5 Languages for Syntax Highlighting\n \n Added 11 new languages to syntax highlighting, completing the roadmap phases 3-5:\n \n```",

Entry point (~20 lines)\n78: ├── lib.rs               # Library root with module exports\n79: ├── app.rs               # App struct, ApplicationHandler impl\n80: ├── input.rs             # handle_key, keyboard→Msg mapping\n81: ├── view.rs              # Renderer, drawing functions\n82: ├── perf.rs              # PerfStats, debug overlay (debug only)\n83: ├── model/\n84: │   ├── mod.rs           # AppModel struct, layout constants\n85: │   ├── document.rs      # UiState (cursor blink, transient messages)\n89: │   └── status_bar.rs    # StatusBar, StatusSegment, sync_status_bar()\n90: ├── update/\n91: │   ├── mod.rs           # Pure dispatcher\n92: │   ├── editor.rs        # Cursor, selection, expand/shrink\n93: │   ├── document.rs      # Text editing, undo/redo\n94: │   ├── layout.rs        # Split views, tabs, groups\n95: │   ├── app.rs           # File operations, window resize\n96: │   └── ui.rs            # 2025-12-15\n76: \n77: ### Added - Incremental Parsing with tree.edit()\n78: \n79: Implemented proper incremental parsing for significantly faster syntax highlighting on edits:\n80: \n81: **Tree Caching:**\n82: - `ParserState` now caches parsed trees per document in `doc_cache: HashMap<DocumentId, DocParseState>`\n83: - Each `DocParseState` stores the tree, source text, and language\n84: - Cache enables incremental parsing on subsequent edits\n85: \n86: **Edit Diffing:**\n87: - `compute_incremental_edit()` computes `InputEdit` by diffing old vs new source\n88: - Finds common prefix/suffix to minimize the edited region\n89: - `byte_to_point()` converts byte offsets to tree-sitter `Point` (row, column)\n90: \n91: **Incremental Parse Flow:**\n92: 1. On edit, diff cached source against new source\n93: 2. Call `tree.edit(&input_edit)` on cached tree\n94: 3. Pass edited tree to `parser.parse(source, Some(&old_tree))`\n95: 4. Tree-sitter reuses unchanged nodes, only re-parsing the changed region\n96: \n97: **Performance Results:**\n98: | File Size | Full Reparse | Incremental |\n99: |-----------|--------------|-------------|\n100: | 100 lines | 67µs | 68µs |\n101: | 500 lines | 330µs | 356µs |\n102: | 1000 lines | 660µs | 728µs |\n103: | 5000 lines | 3.4ms | 3.6ms |\n104: \n105: *Note: Incremental is similar speed due to highlight extraction dominating; benefit increases for larger files.*\n106: \n107: **New Benchmark Suite** (`benches/syntax.rs`):\n108: - `parse_sample` - Parse small samples for each language\n109: - `parse_only_sample` - Isolated parse time (pre-initialized parser)\n110: - `parse_only_large_rust` - Large file scaling (100-10000 lines)\n111: - `incremental_parse_small_edit` - Incremental with append\n112: - `incremental_parse_middle_edit` - Incremental with mid-file edit\n113: - `full_reparse_comparison` - Fresh parser baseline\n114: \n115: **New Makefile Target:**\n116: - `make bench-syntax` - Run syntax highlighting benchmarks\n117: \n118: ### Changed\n119: \n120: - `ParserState` now includes `doc_cache` for tree caching\n121: - `parse_and_highlight()` uses cached trees for incremental parsing\n122: - Added `clear_doc_cache()` method for document cleanup\n123: \n124: ---\n125: \n126: ## v0.3.1 - 2025-12-15\n127: \n128: ### Fixed - Syntax Highlighting Bugs\n129: \n130: Critical fixes for the syntax highlighting system:\n131: \n132: **Tree-sitter Incremental Parsing Bug:**\n133: - Fixed misaligned highlights after document edits (e.g., pressing Enter)\n134: - Root cause: Passing old cached tree to tree-sitter without calling `tree.edit()`\n135: - Tree-sitter incorrectly reused nodes from stale tree, producing wrong line/column positions\n136: - Fix: Always do full reparse by passing `None` instead of cached tree\n137: - Removed unused tree caching from `ParserState` until proper incremental parsing is implemented\n138: \n139: **Flash of Unstyled Text (FOUC):**\n140: - Fixed jarring unstyled flash during syntax re-parsing\n141: - Old highlights are now preserved until new ones arrive\n142: - Revision checks ensure only matching highlights are applied\n143: - Reduced debounce from 50ms to 30ms for snappier updates\n144: \n145: **Tab Expansion in Highlighting:**\n146: - [Executive Summary](#executive-summary)\n15: 2. [Current Architecture Analysis](#current-architecture-analysis)\n16: 3. [Identified Issues](#identified-issues)\n17: 4. [References](#references)\n22: \n23: ---\n24: \n25: ## Executive Summary\n26: \n27: ### What Works Today\n28: \n29: - Font rendering scales correctly (14pt * scale_factor)\n30: - Window dimensions are correctly handled in physical pixels\n31: - Mouse input is correctly received in physical pixels\n32: - Character widths and line heights are correctly computed from scaled fonts\n33: \n34: ### What's Broken\n35: \n36: - **UI layout constants don't scale**: `TAB_BAR_HEIGHT`, `SPLITTER_WIDTH`, padding values are hardcoded in pixels\n37: - **Scale factor isn't stored**: Can't be used after initialization\n38: - **No scale factor change handling**: Moving window between monitors breaks UI\n39: - **Inconsistent coordinate types**: No semantic distinction between physical/logical pixels\n40: \n41: ### Impact\n42: \n43: On a 2x Retina display:\n44: - Tab bar appears half the intended size (28px physical = 14pt logical)\n45: - Gutter padding appears too tight\n46: - Click targets for tabs may be misaligned\n47: - Overall UI looks cramped while text looks correct\n48: \n49: ---\n50: \n51: ## Current Architecture Analysis\n52: \n53: ### 1. Scale Factor Retrieval (Correct)\n54: \n55: **Location:** `src/view/mod.rs:42-58`\n56: \n57: ```rust\n58: impl Renderer {\n59:     pub fn new(window: Rc<Window>, ...) -> Result<Self> {\n60:         let scale_factor = window.scale_factor();  // Correctly retrieved\n61:         // ...\n62:         let font_size = 14.0 * scale_factor as f32;  // Font correctly scaled\n63:         // ...\n64:     }\n65: }\n66: ```\n67: \n68: The scale factor is retrieved correctly from winit, and font size is properly scaled. However, the scale factor is **not stored** for later use.\n69: \n70: ### 2. Window Coordinates (Physical Pixels Throughout)\n71: \n72: Winit provides everything in **physical pixels**:\n73: \n74: | Event | Type | Unit |\n75: |-------|------|------|\n76: | `WindowEvent::Resized` | `PhysicalSize<u32>` | Physical pixels |\n77: | `WindowEvent::CursorMoved` | `PhysicalPosition<f64>` | Physical pixels |\n78: | `window.inner_size()` | `PhysicalSize<u32>` | Physical pixels |\n79: \n80: **Source:** [winit documentation](https://docs.rs/winit/latest/winit/event/enum.WindowEvent.html)\n81: \n82: The code correctly uses these physical values throughout rendering.\n83: \n84: ### 3. Hardcoded Layout Constants (THE BUG)\n85: \n86: **Problematic constants:**\n87: \n88: | Constant | Location | Current Value | Issue |\n89: |----------|----------|---------------|-------|\n90: | `TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` | `src/view/geometry.rs:18` | `28` | Not scaled |\n91: | `SPLITTER_WIDTH` | `src/model/editor_area.rs:602` | `6.0` | Not scaled |\n92: | `GUTTER_PADDING_PX` | `src/model/mod.rs:224` | `4.0` | Not scaled |\n93: | `TEXT_AREA_PADDING_PX` | `src/model/mod.rs:226` | `8.0` | Not scaled |\n94: \n95: **Inline hardcoded values in `src/view/mod.rs`:**\n96: - Tab padding: `rect_x + 4`, `tab_x + 8`, `tab_y + 4`\n97: - Tab gap: `tab_width + 2`, `+ 16` for text padding\n98: - Border width: `1` pixel\n99: \n100: These constants work correctly on 1x displays but produce half-sized UI elements on 2x Retina displays because:\n101: - Font renders at 28pt (14 * 2) - takes up 2x physical pixels per logical pixel\n102: - Tab bar is 28 physical pixels (but should be 56 for correct proportions)\n103: \n104: ### 4. Window Creation (Logical → Physical Conversion)\n105: \n106: **Location:** `src/runtime/app.rs:877-881`\n107: \n108: ```rust\n109: let window_attributes = Window::default_attributes()\n110:     .with_title(\"Token\")\n111:     .with_inner_size(LogicalSize::new(800, 600));  // Logical size\n112: ```\n113: \n114: The window is created with a **logical size**, which winit correctly converts to physical size based on the display's scale factor. This is correct behavior.\n115: \n116: ### 5. Missing Event Handler\n117: \n118: No handler for `WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged`:\n119: - If user drags window to a monitor with different DPI, the UI won't adapt\n120: - The renderer would need to reinitialize font metrics and layout\n121: \n122: ---\n123: \n124: ## Identified Issues\n125: \n126: ### Issue 1: UI Elements Don't Scale with Display DPI\n127: \n128: **Severity:** High\n129: **Affected Users:** All Retina/HiDPI display users\n130: \n131: **Symptoms:**\n132: - Tab bar appears too short\n133: - Gutter padding appears cramped\n134: - Overall UI feels \"tight\" compared to text\n135: \n136: **Root Cause:** Layout constants are in physical pixels but aren't multiplied by scale factor.\n137: \n138: ### Issue 2: No Scale Factor Storage\n139: \n140: **Severity:** Medium\n141: \n142: **Root Cause:** Scale factor is retrieved at Renderer init but never stored. It's needed for:\n143: - Scaling layout constants\n144: - Converting between coordinate systems\n145: - Recreating layout after scale factor change\n146: \n147: ### Issue 3: No Scale Factor Change Handling\n148: \n149: **Severity:** Medium\n150: **Affected:** Users with multiple monitors of different DPI\n151: \n152: **Root Cause:** Missing handler for `WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged`.\n153: \n154: ### Issue 4: Inconsistent Coordinate Types\n155: \n156: **Severity:** Low (code quality)\n157: \n158: The codebase uses multiple types without clear semantic meaning:\n159: - `f32` for some dimensions\n160: - `f64` for mouse positions\n161: - `usize` for rendering pixels\n162: - `u32` for window sizes\n163: \n164: No type-level distinction between physical and logical pixels.\n165: \n166: ---\n167: \n168: ## Proposed Solution\n169: \n170: ### Strategy: Physical Pixels with Scaled Constants\n171: \n172: Continue working entirely in **physical pixels** (current approach), but:\n173: 1. Store the scale factor\n174: 2. Scale all layout constants by the scale factor\n175: 3. Handle scale factor changes\n176: \n177: This minimizes refactoring while fixing the core issues.\n178: \n179: ### Why Not Logical Pixels?\n180: \n181: Working in logical pixels would require:\n182: - Converting all mouse input from physical to logical\n183: - Converting all rendering output from logical to physical\n184: - More places for conversion bugs\n185: \n186: Since rendering (softbuffer) works in physical pixels and winit provides physical coordinates, staying in physical pixels is simpler.\n187: \n188: ### Core Changes\n189: \n190: #### 1. Store Scale Factor in Model\n191: \n192: ```rust\n193: // src/model/mod.rs\n194: \n195: pub struct AppModel {\n196:     // ... existing fields ...\n197: \n198:     /// Display scale factor (1.0 for standard, 2.0 for retina)\n199:     pub scale_factor: f64,\n200: }\n201: ```\n202: \n203: #### 2. Add Scaled Layout Metrics\n204: \n205: ```rust\n206: // src/model/mod.rs (new)\n207: \n208: /// Layout metrics scaled for the current display\n209: #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy)]\n210: pub struct ScaledMetrics {\n211:     /// Tab bar height in physical pixels\n212:     pub tab_bar_height: usize,\n213:     /// Splitter width in physical pixels\n214:     pub splitter_width: f32,\n215:     /// Gutter padding in physical pixels\n216:     pub gutter_padding: f32,\n217:     /// Text area padding in physical pixels\n218:     pub text_area_padding: f32,\n219:     /// Standard UI padding (small) in physical pixels\n220:     pub padding_small: usize,\n221:     /// Standard UI padding (medium) in physical pixels\n222:     pub padding_medium: usize,\n223: }\n224: \n225: impl ScaledMetrics {\n226:     /// Base values at scale factor 1.0\n227:     const BASE_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT: f64 = 28.0;\n228:     const BASE_SPLITTER_WIDTH: f64 = 6.0;\n229:     const BASE_GUTTER_PADDING: f64 = 4.0;\n230:     const BASE_TEXT_AREA_PADDING: f64 = 8.0;\n231:     const BASE_PADDING_SMALL: f64 = 2.0;\n232:     const BASE_PADDING_MEDIUM: f64 = 4.0;\n233: \n234:     pub fn new(scale_factor: f64) -> Self {\n235:         Self {\n236:             tab_bar_height: (Self::BASE_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT * scale_factor).round() as usize,\n237:             splitter_width: (Self::BASE_SPLITTER_WIDTH * scale_factor) as f32,\n238:             gutter_padding: (Self::BASE_GUTTER_PADDING * scale_factor) as f32,\n239:             text_area_padding: (Self::BASE_TEXT_AREA_PADDING * scale_factor) as f32,\n240:             padding_small: (Self::BASE_PADDING_SMALL * scale_factor).round() as usize,\n241:             padding_medium: (Self::BASE_PADDING_MEDIUM * scale_factor).round() as usize,\n242:         }\n243:     }\n244: }\n245: ```\n246: \n247: #### 3. Update Model to Include Scaled Metrics\n248: \n249: ```rust\n250: // src/model/mod.rs\n251: \n252: pub struct AppModel {\n253:     // ... existing fields ...\n254:     pub scale_factor: f64,\n255:     pub metrics: ScaledMetrics,\n256: }\n257: \n258: impl AppModel {\n259:     pub fn new(window_width: u32, window_height: u32, scale_factor: f64, ...) -> Self {\n260:         let metrics = ScaledMetrics::new(scale_factor);\n261:         // ... rest of initialization\n262:     }\n263: \n264:     /// Update scale factor and recalculate metrics\n265:     pub fn set_scale_factor(&mut self, scale_factor: f64) {\n266:         self.scale_factor = scale_factor;\n267:         self.metrics = ScaledMetrics::new(scale_factor);\n268:         // Also need to update font metrics via renderer\n269:     }\n270: }\n271: ```\n272: \n273: #### 4. Handle Scale Factor Changes\n274: \n275: ```rust\n276: // src/runtime/app.rs\n277: \n278: WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged { scale_factor, inner_size_writer } => {\n279:     // Update model\n280:     update(&mut self.model, Msg::App(AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged(scale_factor)));\n281: \n282:     // Reinitialize renderer with new font size\n283:     if let Some(window) = &self.window {\n284:         if let Some(context) = &self.context {\n285:             self.renderer = Some(Renderer::new(Rc::clone(window), context, scale_factor)?);\n286:             // Sync char_width and line_height to model\n287:             if let Some(renderer) = &self.renderer {\n288:                 update(&mut self.model,\n289:                     Msg::App(AppMsg::FontMetricsChanged(\n290:                         renderer.char_width(),\n291:                         renderer.line_height()\n292:                     ))\n293:                 );\n294:             }\n295:         }\n296:     }\n297: \n298:     Some(Cmd::Redraw)\n299: }\n300: ```\n301: \n302: #### 5. Update Renderer to Accept Scale Factor\n303: \n304: ```rust\n305: // src/view/mod.rs\n306: \n307: impl Renderer {\n308:     pub fn new(window: Rc<Window>, context: &softbuffer::Context<Rc<Window>>) -> Result<Self> {\n309:         let scale_factor = window.scale_factor();\n310:         Self::with_scale_factor(window, context, scale_factor)\n311:     }\n312: \n313:     pub fn with_scale_factor(\n314:         window: Rc<Window>,\n315:         context: &softbuffer::Context<Rc<Window>>,\n316:         scale_factor: f64\n317:     ) -> Result<Self> {\n318:         let font_size = 14.0 * scale_factor as f32;\n319:         // ... existing initialization ...\n320:     }\n321: }\n322: ```\n323: \n324: #### 6. Replace Hardcoded Constants\n325: \n326: Replace all uses of hardcoded constants with `model.metrics.*`:\n327: \n328: ```rust\n329: // Before (src/view/mod.rs)\n330: frame.fill_rect_px(rect_x, rect_y, rect_w, TAB_BAR_HEIGHT, tab_bar_bg);\n331: let tab_x = rect_x + 4;\n332: \n333: // After\n334: frame.fill_rect_px(rect_x, rect_y, rect_w, model.metrics.tab_bar_height, tab_bar_bg);\n335: let tab_x = rect_x + model.metrics.padding_medium;\n336: ```\n337: \n338: ---\n339: \n340: ## Implementation Plan\n341: \n342: ### Phase 1: Add Infrastructure (30 min)\n343: \n344: 1. Add `scale_factor: f64` field to `AppModel`\n345: 2. Add `ScaledMetrics` struct with all layout constants\n346: 3. Add `metrics: ScaledMetrics` field to `AppModel`\n347: 4. Update `AppModel::new()` to accept scale factor\n348: 5. Add `AppMsg::ScaleFactorChanged(f64)` message variant\n349: 6. Add `AppMsg::FontMetricsChanged(f32, usize)` message variant\n350: \n351: **Files:** `src/model/mod.rs`, `src/messages.rs`\n352: \n353: ### Phase 2: Update App Initialization (30 min)\n354: \n355: 1. Pass scale factor from Renderer to App on init\n356: 2. Handle `WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged`\n357: 3. Reinitialize renderer on scale factor change\n358: 4. Sync font metrics back to model\n359: \n360: **Files:** `src/runtime/app.rs`, `src/view/mod.rs`\n361: \n362: ### Phase 3: Replace Hardcoded Values (1-2 hours)\n363: \n364: 1. Replace `TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` with `model.metrics.tab_bar_height`\n365: 2. Replace `SPLITTER_WIDTH` with `model.metrics.splitter_width`\n366: 3. Replace `GUTTER_PADDING_PX` with `model.metrics.gutter_padding`\n367: 4. Replace `TEXT_AREA_PADDING_PX` with `model.metrics.text_area_padding`\n368: 5. Replace inline padding values (`+ 4`, `+ 8`, `+ 16`, etc.)\n369: 6. Update geometry helpers to accept metrics\n370: \n371: **Files:**\n372: - `src/view/mod.rs` (tab bar, gutter rendering)\n373: - `src/view/geometry.rs` (hit testing)\n374: - `src/model/editor_area.rs` (splitter calculations)\n375: - `src/model/mod.rs` (gutter calculations)\n376: \n377: ### Phase 4: Update Tests (30 min)\n378: \n379: 1. Update test helpers to include scale factor\n380: 2. Add tests for scaled metrics calculation\n381: 3. Verify hit testing works at different scale factors\n382: \n383: **Files:** `tests/common/mod.rs`, `tests/layout.rs`\n384: \n385: ### Phase 5: Documentation (15 min)\n386: \n387: 1. Update CLAUDE.md if needed\n388: 2. Add comments explaining coordinate systems\n389: 3. Archive this document in docs/archived/ after implementation\n390: \n391: ---\n392: \n393: ## Coordinate System Guidelines\n394: \n395: ### For Future Development\n396: \n397: 1. **All rendering coordinates are in physical pixels**\n398:    - Framebuffer dimensions\n399:    - Drawing positions\n400:    - Rectangle bounds\n401: \n402: 2. **All layout constants should use `ScaledMetrics`**\n403:    - Never hardcode pixel values in rendering code\n404:    - Define base values at scale factor 1.0\n405:    - Multiply by scale factor to get physical pixels\n406: \n407: 3. **Window creation uses logical pixels**\n408:    - `LogicalSize::new(800, 600)` - winit converts to physical\n409: \n410: 4. **Mouse input is in physical pixels**\n411:    - `CursorMoved.position` is `PhysicalPosition<f64>`\n412:    - No conversion needed for hit testing\n413: \n414: 5. **Document coordinates are scale-independent**\n415:    - Line numbers, column numbers\n416:    - Selection ranges\n417:    - Cursor positions\n418: \n419: ### Type Conventions (Recommended for Future Refactor)\n420: \n421: Consider adding type aliases for clarity:\n422: \n423: ```rust\n424: /// Physical pixel coordinate (actual display pixels)\n425: type PhysicalPx = f64;\n426: \n427: /// Logical point (at scale factor 1.0, multiply by scale_factor for physical)\n428: type LogicalPt = f64;\n429: ```\n430: \n431: ---\n432: \n433: ## Testing Strategy\n434: \n435: ### Manual Testing Checklist\n436: \n437: - [ ] Launch on standard 1x display - UI should look unchanged\n438: - [ ] Launch on 2x Retina display - UI elements should be correctly sized\n439: - [ ] Drag window from 1x to 2x display - UI should adapt\n440: - [ ] Drag window from 2x to 1x display - UI should adapt\n441: - [ ] Tab clicks work correctly on both displays\n442: - [ ] Gutter clicks (line numbers) work correctly\n443: - [ ] Splitter drag works correctly\n444: - [ ] Text cursor positioning matches clicks\n445: \n446: ### Automated Tests\n447: \n448: ```rust\n449: #[test]\n450: fn test_scaled_metrics_standard() {\n451:     let metrics = ScaledMetrics::new(1.0);\n452:     assert_eq!(metrics.tab_bar_height, 28);\n453:     assert_eq!(metrics.splitter_width, 6.0);\n454: }\n455: \n456: #[test]\n457: fn test_scaled_metrics_retina() {\n458:     let metrics = ScaledMetrics::new(2.0);\n459:     assert_eq!(metrics.tab_bar_height, 56);\n460:     assert_eq!(metrics.splitter_width, 12.0);\n461: }\n462: \n463: #[test]\n464: fn test_scaled_metrics_fractional() {\n465:     // Windows uses 1.25, 1.5 scales\n466:     let metrics = ScaledMetrics::new(1.5);\n467:     assert_eq!(metrics.tab_bar_height, 42);  // 28 * 1.5 = 42\n468: }\n469: ```\n470: \n471: ---\n472: \n473: ## References\n474: \n475: ### winit Documentation\n476: \n477: - [WindowEvent::CursorMoved](https://docs.rs/winit/latest/winit/event/enum.WindowEvent.html) - Returns `PhysicalPosition<f64>`\n478: - [WindowEvent::Resized](https://docs.rs/winit/latest/winit/event/enum.WindowEvent.html) - Returns `PhysicalSize<u32>`\n479: - [WindowEvent::ScaleFactorChanged](https://docs.rs/winit/latest/winit/event/enum.WindowEvent.html) - DPI change handling\n480: \n481: ### Related Issues\n482: \n483: - [winit#1371](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues/1371) - Historical bug with CursorMoved on macOS\n484: - [winit#1406](https://github.com/rust-windowing/winit/issues/1406) - LogicalPosition vs PhysicalPosition inconsistency\n485: \n486: ### Internal Documentation\n487: \n488: - [EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md](./EDITOR_UI_REFERENCE.md) - Chapter 10.6 covers High-DPI\n489: - [DAMAGE-TRACKING.md](./feature/DAMAGE-TRACKING.md) - Related rendering optimization\n490: - [TEXT_PHYSICS_LAYER.md](./feature/TEXT_PHYSICS_LAYER.md) - Coordinate systems for cursor/viewport\n491: \n492: ---\n493: \n494: ## Summary\n495: \n496: The current implementation correctly handles font scaling but fails to scale UI layout constants. The fix involves:\n497: \n498: 1. Storing the scale factor in the model\n499: 2. Creating a `ScaledMetrics` struct with all layout constants\n500: 3. Replacing hardcoded pixel values throughout the codebase\n501: 4. Handling scale factor changes when moving between monitors\n502: \n503: This maintains the current \"physical pixels everywhere\" approach while ensuring all UI elements scale correctly with the display.",

  "content": "1: # Implementer's Guide to Modern Text Editor UI Geometry, Mathematics, Edge Cases, and Problems\n2: \n3: A comprehensive reference for building scrollable text editing interfaces with tabs, split panels, soft wrapping,\n4: autocomplete overlays, and all the coordinate transformations in between.\n5: \n6: > **Target Audience:** Desktop software developers building code editors in Rust, Go, C++, or similar languages.\n7: > This guide assumes monospace fonts and left-to-right text direction. Web-specific concerns (DOM, CSS) are\n8: > mentioned only where relevant for conceptual understanding.\n9: \n10: ---\n11: \n12: ## Table of Contents\n13: \n14: 1. [Foundational Concepts: The Scrollable Region](#chapter-1-foundational-concepts-the-scrollable-region)\n15: 2. [Structural Hierarchy: Tabs, Splits, and Editor Groups](#chapter-2-structural-hierarchy-tabs-splits-and-editor-groups)\n16: 3. [The Gutter System](#chapter-3-the-gutter-system)\n17: 4. [Viewport Geometry and Line Calculations](#chapter-4-viewport-geometry-and-line-calculations)\n18: 5. [Cursor-Viewport Interaction Model](#chapter-5-cursor-viewport-interaction-model)\n19: 6. [Soft Wrapping: The Coordinate System Split](#chapter-6-soft-wrapping-the-coordinate-system-split)\n20: 7. [Autocomplete and Overlay Positioning](#chapter-7-autocomplete-and-overlay-positioning)\n21: 8. [Syntax Highlighting Integration](#chapter-8-syntax-highlighting-integration)\n22: 9. [The Complete Render Pipeline](#chapter-9-the-complete-render-pipeline)\n23: 10. [Edge Cases and Special Considerations](#chapter-10-edge-cases-and-special-considerations)\n24: 11. [Naming Conventions and Terminology](#chapter-11-naming-conventions-and-terminology)\n25: 12. [Cursor Styles: Pipe, Block, and Underline](#chapter-12-cursor-styles-pipe-block-and-underline)\n26: 13. [Keyboard Mapping and Command Dispatch](#chapter-13-keyboard-mapping-and-command-dispatch)\n27: 14. [Mouse Interaction and Drag Selection](#chapter-14-mouse-interaction-and-drag-selection)\n28: \n29: **Appendices:**\n30: \n31: - [A: Event Flow Example](#appendix-a-event-flow-example)\n32: - [B: Coordinate Transformation Functions](#appendix-b-coordinate-transformation-functions)\n33: - [C: Binary Search Helpers](#appendix-c-binary-search-helpers)\n34: - [D: Useful Constants and Defaults](#appendix-d-useful-constants-and-defaults)\n35: - [E: Unicode and UTF-8 Considerations for Code Editors](#appendix-e-unicode-and-utf-8-considerations-for-code-editors)\n36: - [F: Right-to-Left and Bidirectional Text Theory](#appendix-f-right-to-left-and-bidirectional-text-theory)\n37: \n38: ---\n39: \n40: ## Chapter 1: Foundational Concepts: The Scrollable Region\n41: \n42: ### 1.1 Core Entities\n43: \n44: At the most fundamental level, a scrollable region consists of these conceptual components:\n45: \n46: #### Content (Document / Scrollable Content)\n47: \n48: The full extent of what _could_ be displayed. Has its own coordinate system, often called **content coordinates** or\n49: **document coordinates**. For a text editor, this might be 50,000 pixels tall even though only 800 pixels are visible at\n50: any moment.\n51: \n52: #### Viewport (View / Visible Region)\n53: \n54: The rectangular \"window\" through which you see a portion of the content. Has a fixed size (width × height) and its own\n55: coordinate system where (0,0) is typically the top-left corner of what's currently visible.\n56: \n57: #### Scroll Offset (Scroll Position)\n58: \n59: The translation between content coordinates and viewport coordinates. Typically expressed as `(scrollX, scrollY)` — how\n60: far into the content the viewport's top-left corner is positioned.\n61: \n62: #### Scrollable Extent (Scroll Range / Scroll Bounds)\n63: \n64: The maximum distance the content can scroll. Always clamped to non-negative values:\n65: \n66: ```\n67: scrollableHeight = max(0, contentHeight - viewportHeight)\n68: scrollableWidth  = max(0, contentWidth - viewportWidth)\n69: ```\n70: \n71: When `contentHeight <= viewportHeight`, there is no vertical scroll (scrollableHeight = 0).\n72: \n73: ### 1.2 The Scrollbar Anatomy\n74: \n75: The **scrollbar** is the entire control assembly, typically containing:\n76: \n77: | Part           | Alternative Names                | Purpose                                          |\n78: | -------------- | -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |\n79: | **Track**      | Gutter, Trough, Lane             | The full channel the thumb travels along         |\n80: | **Thumb**      | Handle, Knob, Scrubber, Elevator | The draggable indicator of current position      |\n81: | **Buttons**    | Arrows, Steppers                 | Optional increment/decrement buttons at ends     |\n82: | **Page Zones** | Gutter regions                   | Clickable areas above/below thumb for page jumps |\n83: \n84: ### 1.3 Key Mathematical Relationships\n85: \n86: #### Thumb Size (Proportional Scrollbars)\n87: \n88: ```\n89: thumbLength / trackLength = viewportSize / contentSize\n90: ```\n91: \n92: The thumb visually represents what fraction of content is visible.\n93: \n94: **Edge case:** When `contentSize <= viewportSize`, either hide the scrollbar entirely or set `thumbLength = trackLength`\n95: (thumb fills the track, indicating no scrolling possible).\n96: \n97: #### Scroll Position ↔ Thumb Position\n98: \n99: ```\n100: // Only valid when scrollableExtent > 0 and trackLength > thumbLength\n[... omitted lines 101 to 4019 ...]",

}
